THE SAME SHAPE OF GAP, ONE LEVEL UP: `tests/unit/phase8-navigation.test.ts` requires every shell render in a page to be handed the brand context, after Brand Brain and Analytics each rendered the shell twice and passed it to the "no brand selected" branch only — so the Brand Selector vanished the moment a brand was chosen.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | **AND THE SAME DEFECT HAS A SECOND SHAPE.** Where a key is built from a value the DATABASE supplies — an audit entry's actor type, a notification's template key, a publish job's failure code — three screens detected the miss by comparing the translation against the key. `translator` returns `dictionary[key]`, so a miss is `undefined` and never the key: the comparison could not match, the fallback never ran, and `undefined` was rendered as nothing. A failed publish reported no failure. `optionalMessage(locale, key)` answers `string \| null` and is the only correct way to ask for a key the type system cannot know; a guard forbids the comparison shape returning. |
| **D-203** | **A FIXTURE THAT GROWS WITHOUT BOUND EVENTUALLY TESTS THE QUOTA INSTEAD OF THE FEATURE.** The end-to-end automations suite created seven rules per run and removed none, against a configured `maxRulesPerBrand` of twenty, so the third consecutive run failed every authoring test with a limit refusal that had nothing to do with what those tests are about. THE CEILING IS THE PRODUCT WORKING; the fixture was the bug. A seed RESETS what it owns — the analytics seed already did this for its observations, and now does it for the suite's own `E2E `-prefixed rules — rather than the suite being loosened, the ceiling being raised, or the test being quarantined.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |

| **D-204** | **NO PRODUCTION PAYMENT PROVIDER IS CHOSEN IN PHASE 9, AND THE ARCHITECTURE IS FINISHED ANYWAY.** The unresolved provider decision (D-21) is the owner's, and it is NOT permission to pick one on their behalf — so `packages/billing` ships the CONTRACT and one deterministic DEVELOPMENT adapter, and names no vendor anywhere. Three properties make the contract worth more than a vendor would have been at this stage. (1) THERE IS NO METHOD THAT COULD ACCEPT A CARD: not one that refuses an instrument, one that does not exist. `PaymentProviderAdapter` has no parameter anywhere for a PAN, a CVC or a token, which is the only durable way to guarantee PCI scope never arrives inside BrandSpace, and the suite asserts the ABSENCE. (2) CAPABILITIES ARE DECLARED, NEVER ASSUMED EQUAL — a regional provider that cannot prorate or cannot compute tax is read from `capabilities()` rather than discovered at the till, the same discipline the social connectors use. (3) THE DEVELOPMENT ADAPTER IS NOT A STUB THAT RETURNS SUCCESS: it issues a SIGNED event that must be delivered, verified and reconciled, so the honest path is the only path that works and the product cannot be built on the assumption that a redirect is money. Adding a production provider later is a registration in one map plus one adapter file; every route, service, screen and test already speaks only the contract. |
| **D-205** | **A REDIRECT IS NEVER A PAYMENT, AND THE CONFIGURATION CANNOT SAY OTHERWISE.** `commerce.checkout.trustBrowserRedirect` is typed `z.literal(false)`: it exists so the rule is visible in the document an owner reads, and it cannot be switched on by an operator, a migration or a seed. Nothing in the product marks a checkout paid except the reconciler, acting on a signature verified over the RAW body before parsing. The customer-visible consequence is deliberate and is stated in the UI rather than hidden: for a moment after a genuine payment the event has not arrived, and the landing page says "confirming your payment" instead of showing a receipt. A receipt for money that may not have moved is the failure this decision exists to prevent, and "we are still checking" is the honest answer for exactly as long as it is true. |
| **D-206** | **A CUSTOMER'S MFA SEED GETS ITS OWN KEY DOMAIN, WHICH IS WHY THE DASHBOARD MAY HOLD ONE KEY AT LAST.** The second factor is presented at SIGN-IN — a dashboard server action — so whatever key seals an authenticator seed has to be reachable from the login surface. Under `SECRET_VAULT_KEK` that would put every platform provider credential within reach of a login request; under `SOCIAL_TOKEN_VAULT_KEK` it would do the same for every customer's OAuth token. `CUSTOMER_MFA_VAULT_KEK` is therefore a THIRD domain, for the same reason D-136 made a second: one key, one blast radius, and this radius is authenticator seeds and nothing else. The envelope is bound to one user as authenticated data, so material copied onto another row fails to decrypt rather than authenticating the wrong person with the right authenticator — asserted, not asserted-to. The dashboard still holds no `DATABASE_PLATFORM_URL`, no `SECRET_VAULT_KEK` and no `SOCIAL_TOKEN_VAULT_KEK`, so F-07 is unchanged. |
| **D-207** | **EVERY MONETARY ROW STORES ITS CURRENCY _AND_ THAT CURRENCY'S SCALE.** `1000` is `10.00` SAR and `1.000` KWD, and three of the seven launch currencies are three-digit — so a scale read from the live catalogue at render time would silently re-denominate an invoice issued last year the moment an owner corrected a typo. Amounts are `BIGINT` minor units and no floating-point value is ever the canonical representation of money (§6). `Money` refuses to add two amounts whose currency OR scale differ, which turns the whole class of cross-currency arithmetic bugs into a thrown error at the line that caused it rather than a wrong total three screens away. NOTHING CONVERTS: a plan with no price in the customer's currency is UNAVAILABLE and says which of the two reasons applies, because a runtime rate would manufacture a commercial offer the owner never agreed to (D-08). |
| **D-208** | **THE WEBHOOK RESOLVES ITS WORKSPACE FROM A RELATIONSHIP WE WROTE, AND `NormalizedBillingEvent` HAS NO `workspaceId` FIELD FOR ANYBODY TO REACH FOR.** An event arrives before anyone knows whose it is, so the inbox is PLATFORM-owned and the tenant role has no privilege on it at all — one workspace being able to COUNT another's payment events is a disclosure in itself. The workspace is looked up through `billing_profile`, `checkout_session`, `workspace_subscription` or `invoice`, all mappings BrandSpace created; an event that matches none is kept as `UNRESOLVED` rather than guessed at or dropped, so a misconfiguration is visible instead of silently losing money. An event older than the state it describes is `STALE`: recorded, deliberately not applied. A replay collides on the provider's own event id and changes nothing, INCLUDING the record of what the first delivery did. And an unverified signature writes NOTHING AT ALL — not a row, not an audit event — because "we log every rejected delivery" is how a table becomes an attacker's storage. |
| **D-209** | **INVOICE NUMBERS COME FROM A LOCKED COUNTER TABLE, NOT A SEQUENCE, AND ONLY THE PLATFORM ROLE MAY ADVANCE IT.** A PostgreSQL sequence is not transactional: a rolled-back issue would leave a permanent gap in an accounting series that several of the markets this platform sells in expect to be gapless. The allocator takes a row lock inside the caller's own transaction, so twenty simultaneous issues get twenty distinct numbers and a rolled-back one returns its number to the pool. IT IS AN ORDINARY FUNCTION, NOT `SECURITY DEFINER`, and that was a finding rather than a preference: `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` binds a definer too, so the first version was refused by the very policy protecting the counter — and the D-29 gate then correctly refused a second policy naming another role, because a platform-owned table with a tenant-reachable policy is an escalation path. Issuing an invoice is a SYSTEM act in response to an authoritative provider event, never a customer action, so the tenant role has neither EXECUTE on the function nor any privilege on the table. A stronger property than the one being worked around. |
| **D-210** | **ONBOARDING PROGRESS IS DERIVED FROM THE DATA, NEVER STORED.** There is no `onboarding_progress` table and no step counter. A stored position is a second copy of the truth that drifts the first time anything happens outside the wizard — a brand created from the Brands page, an account connected from Settings, an invitation accepted by a colleague — and then the checklist tells a customer to add their first brand while they are looking at three. Every step is a question asked of the workspace's own rows, which makes the journey resumable by construction (closing the tab, signing in elsewhere, coming back a week later all give the same answer) and honest in both directions: a step completed elsewhere is complete, and a thing later deleted makes its step incomplete again. The checklist POINTS AT the product's own screens rather than duplicating them, because a second place to create a brand is a second place for that behaviour to drift. |

| **D-211** | **A CAPABILITY IS WHAT A MODEL MUST BE ABLE TO DO; A TASK IS WHAT THE PRODUCT WANTS DONE.** Until Phase 10 those were one vocabulary, so an operator configured models one FEATURE at a time and routing could only check MODALITY — and modality cannot tell a vision model from a plain text model, because both are `text`. `AI_CAPABILITIES` names the missing noun. Each task declares the capability it needs, each model declares the capabilities it can serve AND the feature flags that back the declaration up, and routing refuses any model that fails the comparison — primary, fallback, or promoted by a profile alike. Checked TWICE, at activation and at request time, because a model can be disabled after a route was activated and because discovering an impossible route on a customer request means the customer discovers it too. THE TASK RULES ARE UNCHANGED AND STILL WIN OUTRIGHT: the capability layer is what answers when nobody wrote a task rule, so every existing route resolves exactly as before. |
| **D-212** | **A ROUTING PROFILE RANKS THE CATALOGUE THE OWNER ENTERED; IT IS NEVER A SAVED LIST OF MODELS.** Economy, Balanced and Premium could not be stored sets of model keys without this repository naming vendors, which CLAUDE.md §2.2 forbids outright. They are RANKING RULES: cheapest suitable, highest-quality suitable, and — for Balanced — a split that tips toward quality when the CAPABILITY declares a requirement, derived from the requirement itself rather than from a hard-coded list of capability names. Every profile chooses only from models already declared for the capability, so no profile can promote a model that cannot do the work; `custom` turns ranking off and the route table is the answer. An explicit primary on a route overrides the strategy for that one capability, because an override a ranking could outvote would not be one. |
| **D-213** | **HISTORICAL USAGE KEEPS THE PRICING BASIS IT WAS CHARGED UNDER; TODAY'S PRICES NEVER RECALCULATE YESTERDAY.** `ai_usage_ledger` stores the cost basis at execution and the Phase 10 catalogue adds `pricingEffectiveFrom` as the OPERATOR-FACING record of when the current rates were entered — a date a margin report that suddenly moves can point at. It is deliberately not an input to any calculation: a report that recomputed history from a rate table would make last quarter's margin change when somebody corrected a typo, which is the same class of defect as re-deriving an invoice's currency scale (D-207). |
| **D-214** | **WHICH PROVIDER IS CONFIGURED IS CONFIGURATION; WHETHER IT WORKS IS AN OBSERVATION, AND THE TWO LIVE IN DIFFERENT PLACES.** The Integrations Hub joins three sources and owns none: the configuration service holds the selection, the settings and the credential REFERENCES, versioned and rollback-able; the Secret Service holds masked metadata; and `integration_health_check` — platform-owned, with no tenant access at all — holds what happened the last time we called. Putting the observation in configuration would turn every health check into a configuration change with an author and an activation; putting the configuration in a table would be the second configuration system §3 forbids. THE HUB LISTS ONLY PROVIDERS WITH ADAPTERS, and says on every row that all five are development doubles: listing a vendor with an empty adapter would be choosing the owner's provider by implication, which D-204 declined to do. Activation goes through `ConfigurationService`, so it inherits the permission model, MFA policy, validation, audit trail and rollback rather than reimplementing any of them. |
| **D-215** | **NO DEVELOPMENT DOUBLE CAN BE CONSTRUCTED IN PRODUCTION — NOT MERELY UNREGISTERED.** The deterministic AI provider and the outbox email provider now REFUSE AT THEIR CONSTRUCTOR, and the registry refuses any development-only integration for production selection in one function every caller shares. Registration was already conditional for the AI mock in three separate files, which is three places to add a fourth and get it wrong. `createEmailProvider` additionally REVERSES its default in production: there is nothing safe to fall back to, because the outbox writes `status: 'SENT'` and delivers nothing, so an unconfigured production deployment gets a provider that refuses loudly rather than swallowing every verification link. AND THE ENVIRONMENT CONTRACT IS NOW ACTUALLY RUN: `parseEnv` existed from Phase 1 and nothing but a unit test had ever called it, so every guarantee in it was asserted against a fixture and enforced nowhere. The API and the worker validate at start-up; production throws, development warns. |
| **D-216** | **THE BILINGUAL INVOICE IS A PRINTABLE HTML DOCUMENT; THE SERVER-RENDERED PDF IS ENGLISH ONLY, AND THE REASON IS A LICENCE NOBODY HAS BOUGHT.** Setting Arabic in a PDF requires two things this repository cannot decide: a LICENSED Arabic-capable font to embed (a cost decision, the owner's) and a SHAPING engine to place contextual cursive glyphs and reorder bidirectional text (not something to hand-roll for an accounting document). So Phase 10 ships the complete renderer boundary, a deterministic dependency-free PDF writer proven against its own cross-reference table, and a standalone print-optimised document route that sets BOTH languages correctly — because every browser already has a licensed font and a shaping engine. `DeterministicPdfRenderer.supportedLocales` is `['en']` and an Arabic request is REFUSED with a sentence rather than served as a page of empty rectangles, which looks like a document until somebody opens it. WHAT THE OWNER MUST DECIDE: which Arabic font may be embedded in documents sent to customers, and whether the production engine is a headless browser or a shaping-capable PDF library. |
| **D-217** | **OBSERVABILITY IS PROVIDER-NEUTRAL AND STAYS THAT WAY; NO METRICS BACKEND IS CHOSEN.** Structured logs, correlation ids, spans and a redaction layer are complete and vendor-free. What Phase 10 deliberately did NOT add is a time-series store, so request volume, queue depth over time and latency histograms have no home — and the Control Center says so rather than drawing an empty chart. READINESS IS NOT THE SAME QUESTION AS LIVENESS, and conflating them is how a fleet restarts itself during a brief database blip: liveness checks nothing external, readiness probes the database for real, and a dependency declares whether losing it stops the platform or merely DEGRADES one capability. Losing Redis takes background jobs offline and returns 200, because removing every instance from a load balancer to protect a queue replaces a broken feature with an unreachable product. |
| **D-218** | **THE INTEGRATIONS HUB IS THE OWNER-FACING CONFIGURATION SURFACE; THE CONFIGURATION AND SECRETS PAGES ARE THE LOWER-LEVEL ONES.** Phase 10 shipped a Hub that could SHOW an owner that a credential was missing and then send them to two other screens to fix it — the Configuration page to create the provider record, the Secrets page to set its key — neither of which knows what an integration is. Six screens and a mental map, which is what §22.1 said the Hub replaced, except it had not. The Hub now renders each provider's declared `settingFields` and `credentialFields` as a form and writes through the SAME two services: settings as a configuration draft that is then activated, credentials through the Secret Service. NOTHING MOVED UNDERNEATH — no second configuration system, no second secret store, no new read path for a value, and the generic pages remain for inspection, advanced administration and recovery. Three properties make it safe rather than merely convenient: the parser walks the REGISTRY and reads values out of the submission, so an undeclared key is never looked at; `applyProviderRecord` is structurally incapable of writing `status` or `activeProviderKey`, so saving a key cannot activate a provider; and a field the registry marks `generated` — a webhook URL, which is the address of one of our own routes — renders read-only and is never read from a form, because an input for it is a way to point a payment callback at somebody else's host. |
| **D-219** | **A CONNECTION TEST THAT READS A DIFFERENT CREDENTIAL THAN THE SCREEN SHOWS IS WORSE THAN NO TEST.** The Phase 10 payment tester constructed its adapter from `BILLING_DEV_WEBHOOK_SECRET` while the Hub displayed a `webhookSecret` the owner had entered, so a green tick certified a credential nobody had verified. Test Connection now receives the settings the owner saved and the credential REFERENCES configuration holds, and the tester exchanges those references for values at the adapter boundary — the single sanctioned decryption seam in docs/SECURITY.md §5.1. THE RESOLUTION DELIBERATELY DOES NOT HAPPEN IN `packages/integrations`: a unit guard asserts that package cannot so much as name the operation, and the first attempt at this correction weakened that boundary before the guard caught it. The development payment test now signs a probe with the stored secret and verifies it, so the test fails if the stored credential is not the one signing. Phase 9's automated billing fixtures still sign their own loopback events with the environment variable; that is a runtime concern, it is unchanged, and the two responsibilities are now explicit rather than conflated.
| **D-220** | **THE FIRST PRODUCTION ACCOUNT IS CREATED BY A SEPARATE, INTERACTIVE COMMAND — NEVER BY THE SEED — AND ENROLMENT IS FINISHED BY A PERSON, NOT BY A SCRIPT.** `prisma/seed.ts` builds a development world (two workspaces, Sample Brand, North Star, customer users, memberships, wallets, campaigns, content), and every one of those rows is fiction that a production database cannot distinguish from a customer's real data. So `prisma/bootstrap-production-owner.ts` is a separate file with a separate entry point whose only writes are the permission catalogue, the system roles, ONE `PlatformUser`, that owner's sealed TOTP seed, the hashes of their recovery codes, and the audit event for all of it. `tests/isolation/bootstrap-production-owner.test.ts` runs it against a freshly migrated database and then reads EVERY table in the schema, failing if anything outside that list holds a row — a negative about the whole database is only provable on a database whose entire contents are known. FIVE REFUSALS GUARD IT, all before a row is written: `APP_ENV` and `NODE_ENV` must both be `production` (D-97 — a built Next.js app sets the second regardless, so only the first proves intent); the connection must answer `brandspace_platform`; the vault must be able to seal (F-09 / §25), because discovering otherwise halfway would leave an owner with a password and no second factor, and MFA is mandatory for platform roles (D-27); placeholder values are rejected with the same marker list the environment parser uses; and **stdout and stdin must both be a real TTY**, with deliberately no override flag — the development seed has one, and in production the only correct response to "this is not a terminal" is to stop, because a TOTP seed printed into a log is a permanent second factor for whoever can read that file. **GENERATING A SEED IS NOT ENROLMENT.** It proves nothing about whether the operator scanned it, whether their authenticator accepted it, or whether the two clocks agree — so MFA stays OFF and no recovery codes exist until a live six-digit code, read off the authenticator and verified with the product's own `totpVerifier`, says otherwise. An account marked `mfaEnabled` on the strength of a seed nobody used is an account whose owner finds out at their first sign-in that mandatory MFA has locked them out of their own platform. **A WRONG CODE ENABLES NOTHING, AND THE HALF-FINISHED STATE IS RECOVERABLE.** The defect this replaced: with a seed already sealed and MFA still off — a crash, or exactly the mistyped code above — the command took the enrolment branch anyway and called `createSecret` for a `ref` that already existed, which the unique index refused, so the only command able to finish a half-finished account was the one command guaranteed to fail on it. A resume now ROTATES that record instead: a new version of the same row, which cannot collide, and which means the old seed is never unsealed or shown. **THE STATE MACHINE IS ENUMERATED AND FAILS CLOSED.** `classifyOwner` is a total function over a snapshot, so the gaps are visible and each state is testable without a database. Every state where MFA may be LIVE — enabled with no seed in the vault, enabled against a different reference, a reference recorded while MFA is off, a seed sealed for an address no account holds — is a refusal that writes nothing and names nobody, because repairing it by guessing replaces a working second factor, which is the difference between a bootstrap and an account-takeover primitive. **RE-RUNNING A FINISHED OWNER IS INERT, LITERALLY:** two reads and a return, with no role rewritten, no permission upserted and no audit event appended. The first version synchronised the catalogue and wrote to the trail before asking whether there was anything to do, so "nothing was changed" was false while the documentation said it was true — and when code and documentation disagree it is the code that has to move. An existing password, seed or recovery-code set is never replaced. |
| **D-221** | **THE BILLING INBOX ROW IS CLAIMED, NOT LOCKED — A CONDITIONAL `UPDATE` CARRYING A TOKEN AND A LEASE.** Settlement runs OUTSIDE the inbox row's own transaction, deliberately: a rolled-back apply must still leave the receipt visible, or an operator cannot see that the event arrived at all. That choice is what made the row's unique index guard the ROW and nothing guard the WORK — so once an unsettled event became retryable (the correct half of the atomicity fix), two deliveries of the same event could both read it as unsettled and both settle it. The sharpest case was `invoice.payment_failed`, which writes a `PaymentAttempt` keyed `attempt:<externalEventId>` under `unique(workspaceId, idempotencyKey)`: two concurrent retries collide there, one settles, the other takes a P2002 — and with an unconditional final status write the LOSER'S failure could land after the WINNER'S success, leaving the row saying FAILED over work that had been applied, so the next delivery applied it again. A database lock cannot fix this, because the work outlives the transaction the lock would live in. `#claim` is one `UPDATE ... WHERE id = $1 AND (status IN ('RECEIVED','RETRYABLE') OR (status = 'PROCESSING' AND claimedAt < lease))`: under READ COMMITTED the second copy blocks, re-evaluates its predicate against the committed row, matches nothing, and reports zero rows — so `count === 1` is PROOF of exclusive ownership rather than a hint, decided by PostgreSQL and not by timing. **THE TOKEN IS WHAT MAKES THE RELEASE SAFE**: the final write is `WHERE id = $1 AND claimToken = $2`, so a delivery whose lease expired and was taken over changes nothing on its way out. **THE LEASE IS WHAT STOPS A CLAIM BECOMING A LEAK**: without it a killed container would strand an event in PROCESSING for ever, trading a double-apply defect for a lost-money one. It is set to four times the settlement timeout, because a lease that can expire under a merely slow settlement is a lease that re-introduces the race it exists to close. |
| **D-222** | **THE PROVIDER'S OWN REDELIVERY IS THE RETRY TRANSPORT, AND `FAILED` MEANS A DECISION ABOUT THE MONEY RATHER THAN A FAILURE TO FINISH.** docs/BILLING-AND-CREDITS.md §5 asks for retry with backoff, a dead letter with an alert, and an admin replay tool. The implementation had none of the three: EVERY exception from settlement became `FAILED`, and the route answers 200 to every verified event, so the provider never redelivered. A dropped connection mid-settlement was therefore PERMANENT — charged at the provider, nothing applied here, no retry anywhere in the system — and the inbox row claimed a decision about the money had been taken when none had. The `billing-events` queue existed in `QUEUE_DEFINITIONS` with `maxAttempts: 8` and had no producer and no consumer. **THE SPLIT IS THE FIX.** §20's `FAILED` is a deterministic comparison of rows we hold against an event we were sent, so every redelivery reaches it again and retrying is noise: it stays terminal, at 200, audited CRITICAL. `RETRYABLE` is everything else — a deadlock, a timeout, a lost connection — where nothing was applied because the settlement is atomic, so a redelivery must re-apply it: 503, and the provider's own exponential schedule is the backoff. **CLASSIFICATION DEFAULTS TO RETRYABLE**, from an enumerated set of terminal codes rather than `instanceof AppError`, because misreading a transient failure as terminal loses a customer's money silently while the reverse costs at most `maxAttempts` redeliveries. **NO SECOND QUEUE HOP**, which is the one place this departs from §5's 'process on the queue': the settlement is already atomic and already claimed, so a queue would add a durable handoff between two systems that are both already durable, and a second place for an event to be lost. The retry BUDGET is still the queue definition's, bound to the reconciler by a unit test so the declared policy and the enforced one cannot drift. `DEAD_LETTER` is the exhausted state, terminal and audited CRITICAL so the alert is EMITTED rather than being a row somebody has to go looking for; `replay()` re-runs it from our own stored normalized payload, refuses every settled status so it cannot turn one payment into two, and names the operator who asked. |
| **D-223** | **AN APPROVAL RECORDS WHAT IT WAS GRANTED OVER, AND THE PUBLISHER RECOMPUTES IT BEFORE THE EXTERNAL CALL.** §14.7 says the approval gate is checked twice because approval can be withdrawn in between — and it was, but both checks asked only whether an approval EXISTED and said `APPROVED`. Neither asked what it was approved over. `editVariant` returns an `APPROVED` item to `DRAFT`, which covers the ordinary edit; a `SCHEDULED` item is deliberately left scheduled, because unscheduling somebody's post from an edit handler is worse than the alternative and the calendar owns that edge. So `submit → approve → schedule → edit the caption → publish` sent words nobody had reviewed under a verdict granted to different words, and every row in the audit trail was individually true. `Approval.approvedFingerprint` now stores a per-variant hash of the fields that decide WHAT GETS PUBLISHED — body, hashtags, first comment, link, asset ids, platform, locale — computed inside the same row lock that writes the verdict, and the publish preflight recomputes it from the variant it is about to send. A mismatch is `APPROVAL_REVOKED`, the existing refusal, because it is the same fact. **TITLE, TAGS, PILLAR AND CAMPAIGN ARE DELIBERATELY OUTSIDE THE FINGERPRINT**: they are properties of the work, not of the post, and invalidating an approval over a retitle trains people to re-approve reflexively, which is how a control becomes a formality. **THE FINGERPRINT LIVES IN `@brandspace/shared`** because `@brandspace/social-connectors` deliberately does not depend on `@brandspace/content` (it takes an injected `PublishApprovalGate` rather than buying a package cycle), and two copies of a hashing rule drift — where drift means either "everything is refused" or, far worse, "nothing is". **THE COLUMN IS NULLABLE AND IS NOT BACKFILLED**, and `approvalCoversVariant` FAILS CLOSED on a null: an approval granted before this shipped cannot prove what it covered, and inventing a fingerprint from today's rows would certify exactly the edit this exists to catch. **THE GATE ALSO READS THE RIGHT ROW NOW.** It called `openForItem`, which returns only `PENDING` approvals, so the decided approval a scheduled post actually publishes under was invisible to it; `latestForItem` returns the highest cycle whatever its status, and `openForItem` keeps its `PENDING`-only meaning for the review queue that needs it. **AMENDED BY D-230**: the gate first compared only the per-variant hash, so the whole-item half of this fingerprint was recorded and not enforced. It now compares both. |
| **D-224** | **A POST CAN BE WRITTEN BY A PERSON, AND THAT PATH DOES NOT PASS THROUGH THE AI GATEWAY AT ALL.** Every route into `content_item` created it as the output of a generation: the Studio composed a brief, reserved credits, called a model and wrote the result. A customer who simply wanted to write their own caption had no way to do it, and the workaround — generate something and overwrite it — reserves credits, charges for a result nobody used, and stamps `origin: 'AI'` on human words for ever. `createManualItem` lives on `ContentLibraryService`, the class that F-07 defines as the one WITHOUT a gateway, so the dashboard can call it directly: the separation that exists because the dashboard must never hold the platform database identity is exactly what makes a no-AI authoring path expressible. The item is written with `origin: 'HUMAN'` and a null `aiRequestId`, which are not decoration — every credit report, margin calculation and usage audit reads them. **THE PERMISSION IT TAKES IS `content.create`, WHOSE MEANING THIS CHANGED** — see D-231. Media is resolved through `ContentMediaResolver.resolveForPlatform` BEFORE the row is written, so an unusable attachment fails the authoring call rather than the publish job three days later; the campaign is resolved through the same predicate the Studio uses; and the whole thing is idempotent on `(workspaceId, brandId, createdByUserId, idempotencyKey)`, so a double-submitted form produces one draft. **AND THE DATABASE IS WHAT DECIDES THAT, WITHOUT RAISING.** The read-then-create shape closes a SEQUENTIAL retry and nothing more: two requests in flight under one key both find no row and both insert, and `create` compiles to a plain INSERT, so the loser takes a P2002. Catching it and re-reading cannot work here — `withWorkspace` runs the whole call in one transaction, a constraint violation aborts that transaction, and the recovery read fails with `current transaction is aborted`. `createMany({ skipDuplicates: true })` compiles to `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, which does not raise and does not abort: the loser BLOCKS on the winner's speculative insertion lock, waits for it to commit, and reports zero rows — so `count === 0` is proof of a replay decided by PostgreSQL rather than by timing, and a winner that rolls back simply lets the insert succeed. The loser writes no variant, no audit event and no second row. Same arbiter, same reasoning, as `recordAutomationEvent`. **FILING IT UNDER A CAMPAIGN FOLLOWS THE COMPOSER'S BRAND, NOT THE RAIL'S, AND TAKES THE PERMISSION THAT ALREADY GOVERNS THAT LINK (D-232).** **THE COMPOSER IS THE CALLER, AND IT READS THE CONTROLS ALREADY ON THE SCREEN.** A server action with no caller is the same defect as `ensureCursors` (D-225) in a different package, so "Save without AI" sits in the composer's own action row beside Estimate and Generate, submitting a fieldless form through `form=`: the brand, the channels, the language and the words are the SAME answers the generate button reads, because a second textarea headed "or write it here" would be a second place for one sentence to live and the reader would have to guess which one the button they pressed was going to use. It reuses the composer's EXISTING idempotency key — derived from the ask, not from the click — so a double submit returns the first draft. THIS IS WHAT MAKES THE PRODUCT REACHABLE AT ALL AS CONFIGURED: there is no selected provider (D-13, D-204), the only registered adapter selects retrieved material verbatim rather than emitting the Studio's JSON envelope, and `tests/e2e/content-studio.spec.ts` has asserted since Phase 5B-2 that in a browser the grounded path reaches AC-11.9's honest refusal. Every module downstream of a draft — the calendar, the approval, the publish job, campaign performance — was therefore downstream of a vendor decision nobody has taken. **THE GUARDS ARE HOISTED, NOT COPIED.** `assertPlatforms`, `assertDraftHeadroom`, `resolveDialectFor` and `requireItemForBrandScope` moved from `ContentStudioService` onto the shared base, because the second copy of a tenant rule is where the exception to it gets written. |
| **D-225** | **A CURSOR IS ENSURED BY THE SWEEP THAT USES IT, NOT BY THE CONNECTION THAT NEEDS IT.** `ensureCursors()` was complete, correct and CALLED BY NOTHING: analytics ingestion could not start for any workspace that had connected an account, because the rows the sweep enumerates were only ever created by tests. The obvious repair — create cursors when a connection is established — would have fixed today and not tomorrow: it leaves every account connected before the fix permanently uncovered, it has to be repeated in the OAuth callback, the reconnect path and the re-authorisation path, and each of those is a place to forget. So the sweep ASKS THE QUESTION ITSELF, every run: active connections with no cursor rows at all, distinct by workspace, bounded by the same batch size as the sweep, then `withWorkspace(...)` per workspace so the write is made under the tenant's own RLS context rather than the platform identity that found it. It is idempotent by construction — `ensureIngestionCursors` upserts against the registry — self-healing after a failed migration or a newly registered metric, and it costs one indexed query on a run that is already enumerating due cursors. A workspace whose ensure throws is LOGGED AND SKIPPED, not fatal: one tenant's broken registry state must not stop ingestion for every other tenant. |
| **D-226** | **A TRANSIENT FAILURE DOES NOT OWN AN IDEMPOTENCY KEY FOR EVER.** `#replayIfRecorded` replayed every TERMINAL status, and a timeout is terminal — so one lost connection POISONED that key permanently: the customer pressed generate again, the gateway handed back the recorded failure without calling any provider, and that request could never succeed. The Creative Studio concealed it by minting a fresh key on every click, which is its own defect (a double click charged twice), but the rule was wrong for every caller. Replay is now decided by whether RUNNING IT AGAIN WOULD REACH THE SAME ANSWER: `SUCCEEDED`, `MODERATION_BLOCKED`, and the enumerated deterministic classes (`CONTENT_FILTERED`, `INVALID_REQUEST`, `CONTEXT_TOO_LONG`) replay, because the customer should get that verdict immediately rather than pay for the same refusal on a loop. Everything else — a timeout, a rate limit, a provider briefly unavailable, an auth error an operator has since fixed, `UNKNOWN` — is transient and must be allowed to run again. **IT DEFAULTS TO TRANSIENT, from a list of terminal codes rather than the reverse**, for the reason D-222 gives: misreading a transient failure as terminal makes a customer's request permanently unfulfillable, while the reverse costs one more provider call. **THE FAILED ROW IS KEPT AND RENAMED, NEVER DELETED** — its cost, class and timing are the record of what happened; only the key is moved aside, suffixed with the row's own id so the new name cannot collide. The move is a conditional `updateMany`, so of two racing retries exactly one frees the key and the other re-reads, bounded at three iterations. **NO DOUBLE CHARGE FOLLOWS**, because a failed request already released its reservation in `#releaseAndRecord`: there is nothing outstanding for the superseding attempt to duplicate. The Studio's client guard is the other half — a `useRef` held across the await, because `useState` updates asynchronously and a second click lands before the re-render — and it KEEPS the key on failure, so the retry is the same logical action rather than a new one. |
| **D-227** | **THE LANGUAGE SWITCH KEEPS THE PAGE, AND THE PAGE ARRIVES AS A REQUEST HEADER.** Both language links pointed at `/${locale}`, so a customer reading a post on the calendar, a report, or a half-filled brand profile was returned to the overview — in a product where one of the two languages is RTL and switching is not an exotic action but the normal way half the audience reads anything. A client-side `usePathname` would have solved it for client components only, and the shell is a server component rendered on routes that are mostly server components; a middleware rewrite would have to guess at the target. The middleware already runs on every dashboard request and already forwards headers, so it forwards `x-brandspace-path` — the path and query it actually matched — and the shell reads it through `next/headers` and swaps only the leading locale segment. `switchLocalePath` is a PURE function with the fallback in it: a path that does not begin with a known locale segment yields `/${target}`, so a malformed or absent header degrades to the old behaviour instead of building a broken URL. The same helper produces the sign-out `next` value, because the two answers must not disagree. **A CONSEQUENCE WORTH RECORDING:** making the shell `async` meant a `'use client'` module could no longer import style constants from it without dragging `next/headers` into the client bundle, which failed the build. The style objects moved to `components/customer-styles.ts`, a module with no server imports — the general rule being that shared presentation constants do not belong in a module that touches the request. |
| **D-228** | **ARCHIVING A CAMPAIGN IS A DECISION ABOUT THE CATALOGUE, NOT ABOUT THE CONTENT IN IT.** An archived campaign disappeared from the compose screen's options, which sounds right and is not: a draft already linked to it then rendered with NO campaign selected, and the next save — a save about something else entirely — silently unlinked it. History was destroyed by a dropdown. The options list now appends the draft's OWN campaign when it is archived, so the screen shows the truth and the link survives a round trip; the archived campaign is still not offered to anything that is not already in it. `setContentCampaign` additionally returns early when the requested campaign is the one already stored, because re-asserting an existing link is not a change and must not be refused by a guard aimed at new ones. **ARCHIVE REMAINS NON-DESTRUCTIVE AND STAYS THAT WAY:** no row is deleted, no `campaignId` is nulled, and the content's history reads the same before and after. |
| **D-229** | **A REPAIR PASS THAT ONLY SEES "NEVER PROVISIONED" REPAIRS ONE STATE AND MISSES THE REST.** D-225 gave `ensureIngestionCursors` a caller and asked the sweep for ACTIVE connections with `analyticsCursors: { none: {} }` — no cursor AT ALL. That is exactly one of the states the function was built to fix. A connection holding a PARTIAL set stopped matching the filter and was therefore skipped FOR EVER: an adapter that later declares `supportsPostMetrics`, a `supportedGranularities` that grows a value, one insert that failed, a row an operator or a purge removed. The function could repair the state and the enumeration could not see it, which is the same shape of defect as the function having no caller at all — one layer up. **THE FILTER IS GONE AND A DURABLE CURSOR REPLACES IT**, because simply asking for every ACTIVE connection re-introduces the starvation D-182 names: past `take: batch` a stable ordering returns the same head of the queue on every tick and the tail is never visited. `social_connection.analyticsCursorsEnsuredAt` is nullable and ordered `ASC NULLS FIRST` — the ordering R4-2 already established for `nextAttemptAt` — so a connection nobody has ensured is visited before one ensured an hour ago, every existing row is at the front on the first tick after the migration, and the batch ROTATES. **THE PARK IS WRITTEN IN THE TENANT'S OWN TRANSACTION**, with the ensure it records, so the two commit together (D-186's shape) and a workspace whose ensure throws is not marked done — it returns to the front rather than being lost. **NOTHING IT DOES CAN RESET INGESTION STATE.** The insert is still `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, so an existing cursor keeps its progress, its backoff and its freshness untouched; the pass can only ADD the rows that are missing. Cost is one indexed `LIMIT` on a sweep already reading that table. |
| **D-230** | **AN APPROVAL IS GRANTED OVER THE POST, SO THE CHECK IS OVER THE POST — NOT OVER ONE ROW OF IT.** D-223 stored both halves of the answer: a per-variant hash and an `item` hash that changes when a variant is ADDED or REMOVED. The publish gate compared only `variants[id]`, so the whole-item half was recorded and never enforced, and this published under a verdict nobody gave: approve a two-channel post, add a third channel, publish the first two. The two original variants are untouched, their hashes still match, and nothing refuses — while the reviewer has never seen the third. Removal was the mirror image: a campaign approved as three channels went out as two, unchallenged. `approvalCoversItem` now recomputes the item fingerprint over EVERY variant the item currently has and compares it with the recorded one, AND asks the narrower per-variant question as well — implied by the first today, asked anyway because a future `item` hash that stopped depending on a sibling's contents must not silently widen what a verdict authorizes. The publish preflight therefore reads the item's whole variant set rather than the single row it is about to send. **THE EXCLUSIONS ARE UNCHANGED AND ARE WHAT KEEP THIS FROM BECOMING A FORMALITY**: `title`, `pillar`, `tags` and `campaignId` are properties of the work rather than of the post, none of them changes a character a follower sees, and invalidating an approval over a retitle teaches people to re-approve without reading. **AND THERE IS NO BACKFILL, STILL.** A comment in `content-fingerprint.ts` said the migration backfilled existing approvals, which was never true and contradicted the migration standing beside it; the code, the migration note, the documents and the tests now say the one thing that is true — an approval predating the column carries NULL, authorizes nothing, and must be granted again. A fingerprint derived from today's rows would certify exactly the edit the mechanism exists to catch. |
| **D-231** | **`content.create` IS THE RIGHT TO BRING A DRAFT INTO THE LIBRARY, NOT THE RIGHT TO SPEND.** It was defined as "Generate content with AI (spends credits)" because, when the permission list was drawn, generation was the only way a `content_item` could come into being. D-224 added manual authoring — no model, no reservation, no ledger entry — under this same key, and it is a create in every sense the key is about: a new row in the brand's library, against the draft ceiling, attributed to its author. So the DESCRIPTION had to move, and nothing else did. **SPENDING IS A PROPERTY OF THE GENERATION PATH.** What reserves and settles credits is the AI Gateway; what decides whether a caller may reach it is the entitlement and the wallet, both checked there. Splitting a second permission out to carry the money would put the guard somewhere the money is not, and would silently drop manual authoring for every role holding this key today. **THE GRANTS ARE PINNED BY A TEST**, so relabelling can never be the cover for widening: the same five roles hold it before and after, and the keys that DO always spend (`analytics.explain`, `copilot.use`) keep their warning. **AND IT IS THE RIGHT TO CREATE A DRAFT, NOT TO FILE ONE** — see D-232, which is what stops the broadened description from quietly broadening the authority with it. |
| **D-232** | **A CONTROL THAT DEPENDS ON A CLIENT CHOICE CANNOT BE ANSWERED ONCE, SERVER-SIDE, BEFORE THE CHOICE EXISTS.** D-224 made a campaign selectable while a post is written, and loaded the options for the brand the RAIL had selected. With the rail on "All brands" there is no such brand, so the page sent an empty list — and that is exactly the case where the composer shows its OWN brand selector. Choosing Brand A there left Brand A's campaigns unavailable for ever, because the server had already decided. So the option list is fetched for the brand the composer actually holds, through a permission-guarded server action, when that brand is not the one the page resolved. **ONE BRAND'S CAMPAIGNS, ASKED FOR WHEN THAT BRAND IS CHOSEN** — the alternative, shipping every brand's campaigns to the browser and filtering there, would put another brand's campaign names in the page for the sake of a dropdown, which is the thing the existing draft path is careful never to do. Changing brand CLEARS the selection, because a campaign belongs to one brand and carrying the choice across would file the post somewhere nobody asked for. **THE PERMISSION IS `campaigns.manage`, BECAUSE THAT IS THE EXISTING CONTRACT.** `setContentCampaignAction` has required it since Phase 8, and the create path accepted a `campaignId` under `content.create` alone — so a member could establish an association they could never afterwards CHANGE, through a crafted submission whether or not a control was on the screen. The selector is gated on it, the create path enforces it server-side, and the option list requires it too, because campaign names are tenant data and a caller who could not use an option should not be shown one. The refusal is `NOT_FOUND`, the same code a campaign in another brand gets, so it does not confirm that a campaign by that id exists. **THE ALTERNATIVE READING IS A PRODUCT DECISION, NOT A DETAIL**: `campaigns.read` would also be defensible — filing a post does not edit a campaign — but it is held by three more roles, so adopting it here would hand `content_creator`, `approver` and `analyst` an authority the screen that already does this refuses them. That is the owner's call and it was not taken here. **AND THE CAMPAIGN IS PART OF THE MANUAL REQUEST'S IDENTITY — ONLY THE MANUAL ONE.** The manual-create idempotency key covered brand, words, channels, language and type; without the campaign, "the same post, filed under Campaign B" hashed to the key Campaign A's draft already owned and REPLAYED it, discarding the customer's new choice silently. A double submit of one form still converges; a different campaign is a different ask. **BUT THE COMPOSER HAD ONE KEY FOR TWO ASKS**, and generation shared it — so moving the campaign selector moved the GENERATION key too, and pressing Generate again became a new `ai_request` and a new credit charge for a field `/api/content/generate` neither receives nor stores. The material is now split by what each endpoint actually gets: `generationKeyFor` over brand, brief, channels, language and type, and `manualKeyFor` over the same plus the campaign. Generation was NOT taught about campaigns to make the keys agree — that would widen Phase 2 to make a hash tidy. The derivation lives in a pure module because the property is a property of the derivation, and a browser cannot prove that no journey makes an extra request while a pure function can. **AND THE EMPTY BRAND HAD TO BECOME VISIBLE.** `brandId` starts as '' under D-191, and a `<select>` whose options do not contain its value renders the FIRST option — so on "All brands" the screen named a brand the state had never chosen, and every consequence of the empty state looked like a bug against a control that appeared to hold an answer. An explicit placeholder option makes the control show what the state is; it is not a brand and cannot be submitted as one. |
| **D-237** | **A HELPER WITH NO CALLER IS NOT A FEATURE, AND FINANCIAL HELPERS ARE THE WORST PLACE TO KEEP ONE.** `expireLapsedGrants`, `sweepAbandonedReservations`, `runCycleReset`, `advanceCycle` and `advanceDunning` were all implemented, all tested, and called by nothing outside `tests/`. `SubscriptionService.dueForCycle` — a method whose only purpose is to be a sweep's input — had no caller at all. So in a running deployment the monthly allowance was granted once at plan assignment and never again, credits never expired, an abandoned reservation held a customer's balance for ever, a scheduled downgrade never took effect, `cancelAtPeriodEnd` ended nothing, and a past-due subscription never escalated. None of that is visible from reading the services, which are correct; it is visible only by asking who calls them. `MaintenanceScheduler.sweepFinance` is now the caller, on the purge cadence, and the regression tests assert against the SCHEDULER rather than the helpers — a test against a helper would have passed before this change and after it. | Current execution Phase 3 | `apps/api/src/scheduler.ts`, `tests/isolation/phase3-billing-operations.test.ts` |
| **D-238** | **A CYCLE BOUNDARY IS CROSSED ONCE, AND `cancelAtPeriodEnd` IS A DECISION THE BOUNDARY MUST ACT ON.** `advanceCycle` moved the period unconditionally, so a duplicated tick or a second API instance granted a second month of access and a second month of credits; and it never read `cancelAtPeriodEnd`, so a customer who cancelled was told access continued until the period end and then watched the period roll over for ever. The write is now conditional on the period end the call read, and the boundary produces one of three outcomes — renew, expire a trial, or end a cancellation — of which only the first grants an allowance. A terminal outcome leaves the period where it is, so the row says when access ended rather than claiming one nobody paid for. | Current execution Phase 3 | `packages/entitlements/src/subscription.ts` |
| **D-239** | **A LIMIT THAT ONLY APPEARS IN A FORM AN OPERATOR FILLS IN IS NOT A LIMIT.** `limit.brands` and `limit.social_accounts` were in the plan catalogue, the quota projection, the plan editor and the downgrade impact check, and no code path consulted either — the configuration schema even said, beside the platform's own connection ceiling, that "the plan limit lives in `plans` and is enforced separately", and it was enforced nowhere. Both are now consumed at the point the resource comes into existence, through `createPlanQuota` and the same atomic `usage_counter` every other quota uses, and the connected-account slot is returned on disconnection. The consumption and the business mutation share ONE PostgreSQL transaction, so neither can exist without the other. | Current execution Phase 3 | `packages/entitlements/src/plan-quota.ts`, `tests/isolation/phase3-connection-quota.test.ts`, `tests/e2e/onboarding-first-brand.spec.ts` |
| **D-240** | **"CORRECT BY CONSTRUCTION" IS A CLAIM ABOUT CODE THAT HAS BEEN READ, NOT ABOUT ROWS THAT EXIST — SO SOMETHING HAS TO ASK THE ROWS.** Every materialised financial number is derived from an immutable record written in the same transaction, and nothing checked that the pairs still agreed. `FinancialReconciler` compares the wallet against a ledger replay, against its buckets, and against its open reservations, and every completed pack purchase against the grant it names. IT REPAIRS NOTHING: a drift means an invariant did not hold, and rewriting the materialised value would destroy the evidence and leave the cause. Its first run found that the isolation fixtures had been describing a state the product cannot produce — a reservation and a bucket that both recorded a hold against a wallet that did not — which is exactly the class of thing it exists to find. | Current execution Phase 3 | `packages/billing/src/financial-reconciliation.ts` |
| **D-241** | **AN AUDIT ROW IS NOT AN ALERT, AND A DEAD-LETTERED EVENT THAT NOBODY CAN LIST IS NOT OPERABLE.** `DEAD_LETTER` was reachable, terminal, audited and replayable, and nothing could enumerate it: the schema carries an index described as "listing the dead-letter queue for an operator" and no query used it, while `BillingReconciler.replay()` had no caller anywhere — the documentation said in as many words that the screen for it was not built. The events that need a person are now listed on the Control Center page an operator already opens to ask whether anything is wrong, and can be replayed from there. The reconciler an operator surface builds has NO PROVIDER REGISTRY, so it can finish an event the platform already verified and cannot accept a new one — the Control Center never holds a webhook signing secret. | Current execution Phase 3 | `packages/billing/src/reconcile.ts`, `apps/admin/src/app/[locale]/console/health/` |
| **D-242** | **AN AUDIT RECORD MAY NOT CLAIM SOMETHING THAT DID NOT HAPPEN.** Suspending a subscription wrote `accessWithdrawn: true`, and nothing withdrew any access: `EntitlementService.contextFor` resolves against `Workspace.planKey` and never reads the subscription's status, so a SUSPENDED subscription kept every capability it had. The record now states what actually happened (`subscriptionSuspended`, `dataRetained`, `accessChanged: false`) and what suspension SHOULD take away is raised as D-234 rather than guessed at. The same rule retired two "available when publishing ships" rows on the customer's Plan page, which the same page was contradicting three cards lower. | Current execution Phase 3 | `packages/billing/src/subscriptions.ts`, `apps/dashboard/src/app/[locale]/plan/page.tsx` |
| **D-243** | **THE PRODUCT HAS EXACTLY ONE BRAND-CREATION PATH, AND IT IS REACHABLE ONLY WHILE THE WORKSPACE HAS NO BRAND.** Found by writing the end-to-end test for the new `limit.brands` enforcement: the create form lives in Brand Brain's empty state, and once a brand exists that screen is replaced by the brand's own. Nothing else in the dashboard or the Control Center creates one. So a workspace can hold exactly one brand however many its plan grants, and the only brand refusal the product can currently reach is the FIRST one against a ceiling of none — which is what the test asserts. The enforcement is at the right place and is complete; the missing thing is a customer surface for a second brand, which belongs to the Phase 6 customer UX work and was deliberately not invented here. Recorded rather than papered over, because a test written against a screen that does not exist is the way this stays hidden. | Current execution Phase 3 | `apps/dashboard/src/app/[locale]/brand-brain/page.tsx`, `tests/e2e/onboarding-first-brand.spec.ts` |
| **D-244** | **A PERIOD AND THE ALLOWANCE THAT BELONGS TO IT COMMIT TOGETHER, OR NEITHER DOES.** The sweep advanced the subscription's period in one transaction and granted the new period's credits in another. Between them was a hole a month of credits fell through: the period committed, the grant failed, and because `dueForCycle` selects on the period end the workspace was no longer due — nothing retried it, and nothing recorded that an allowance had been missed. The first version of this phase's own test asserted that outcome as acceptable ("the broken one still advanced its period"), which is how a silently wrong result gets written down as a requirement. A non-terminal boundary is now ONE transaction — `advanceCycleWithin` plus `runCycleResetWithin`, both taking the transaction the scheduler opens — so the customer has the new period and its credits or still has neither. A terminal boundary (a cancellation reaching its period end, a trial expiring) commits on its own and grants zero: there is no next period for an allowance to belong to. | Current execution Phase 3, pre-merge review | `apps/api/src/scheduler.ts`, `packages/entitlements/src/{subscription,credit-ledger}.ts` |
| **D-245** | **A TOTAL QUOTA COUNTS WHAT EXISTS, NOT WHAT ITS COUNTER WAS TOLD ABOUT.** A usage counter records what has been consumed THROUGH it, and the things a `total` dimension counts — brands, connected accounts — predate the day their dimension was wired up. A workspace with four connected accounts and a counter of zero was admitted four more under a limit of five, and two callbacks racing for the last slot could BOTH be admitted because the counter neither of them incremented had ever heard of the other four. Counting and then creating is the read-then-write the usage service exists to avoid, so the count happens inside the consuming transaction, behind the counter row's own lock (`ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE SET x = x` locks the conflicting row), and the admission is `GREATEST(counter, live) + n <= limit` in one statement. `GREATEST` is what makes it idempotent and non-double-counting: a resource already in the counter is also in the live count, and the greater of two is one of them, never their sum. What "occupies a slot" means is declared ONCE beside the dimension, because the first thing that went wrong was a test building its own adapter with no population at all — and passing. `limit.storage_gb` is deliberately excluded: it counts gigabytes with per-upload rounding, not rows, so a sum-of-bytes baseline would be a different number and would make it less correct. | Current execution Phase 3, pre-merge review | `packages/entitlements/src/{usage,plan-quota}.ts` |
| **D-246** | **A SUBSCRIPTION THAT ENDED STOPS GRANTING ITS PLAN, AND NOTHING IS DELETED TO ACHIEVE IT.** `EntitlementService.contextFor` resolved the plan from `Workspace.planKey` and never read the subscription, and the cycle boundary does not clear that column — deliberately, because the commercial record is history. So a CANCELLED or EXPIRED subscription kept every capability it had while the boundary, the billing screen and the audit trail all said the relationship was over; docs/BILLING-AND-CREDITS.md §3.4 says access continues UNTIL the period end, not after it. Those two statuses now resolve as a workspace on NO plan: quotas become none rather than unlimited, plan-granted capabilities fall back to their declared defaults, and everything gated on a permission rather than an entitlement — reading, the billing screen, the invoice documents, the accounting export — is untouched, which is what "data retained, export available" requires of this layer. PAST_DUE is unchanged (§3.5 gives it full access while dunning runs). SUSPENDED is unchanged and stays D-234. | Current execution Phase 3, pre-merge review | `packages/entitlements/src/service.ts` |
| **D-247** | **A CLEAN RECONCILIATION RECORD BELONGS TO A ROTATION, NOT TO A PAGE.** The pass reads the platform a page at a time, and the clean record was written by whichever page happened to finish the rotation — so a rotation whose FIRST page found drift and whose last did not wrote `reconciliation.clean` over the top of its own CRITICAL. The drift record survived, and the newest word on the platform's financial state said everything was fine. `foldRotation` carries "did any page of this rotation find drift" across the pages and resets it only when the cursor wraps. It is a PURE function rather than three lines at the call site, because the sequence that was wrong is exactly the one worth asserting exhaustively and a database is not needed to do it. The state stays in memory with the cursor: a restart re-checks from the top, which is the safe direction, and it can only postpone a clean statement, never lose a finding. | Current execution Phase 3, pre-merge review | `packages/billing/src/financial-reconciliation.ts`, `apps/api/src/scheduler.ts` |
| **D-248** | **A SCHEDULED PLAN CHANGE THAT CANNOT BE RESOLVED DOES NOT CONSUME ITS BOUNDARY.** `advanceCycleWithin` applied a pending plan only when terms for it could be resolved (`applyPending = renews && pendingPlanKey !== null && nextTerms !== null`), and otherwise renewed the CURRENT terms and moved the period anyway. So a downgrade scheduled onto a plan the active catalogue no longer defines — or one with no price in the subscription's billing currency, which D-08 makes a real state because no FX conversion happens at runtime — advanced into another period on the old plan, kept `pendingPlanKey` pointing at a plan that would never arrive, and granted the OLD plan's allowance. Nobody was told, and because `dueForCycle` selects on the period end the workspace was no longer due, so the next sweep would repeat it a month later. A boundary is now refused outright (`CONFLICT`) whenever a renewing subscription carries a pending plan whose terms are absent, or whose terms are for some OTHER plan: the whole boundary — period, plan and allowance alike — rolls back and stays due for an operator to correct the catalogue, exactly as an unresolvable CURRENT plan already did. Substituting the old terms, or any other terms, would infer billing economics nobody approved: what a customer pays after a scheduled downgrade to a plan that no longer exists is a PRODUCT decision, not a fallback for code to pick. The scheduler logs which of the two causes it was — absent from the catalogue, or no price in the billing currency — because an operator's repair differs. | Current execution Phase 3, pre-merge review | `packages/entitlements/src/subscription.ts`, `apps/api/src/scheduler.ts` |
| **D-249** | **ONE RULE DECIDES WHETHER AN IDEMPOTENCY KEY NAMES THE SAME REQUEST, AND BOTH PATHS ASK IT.** A-9 made the immutable request fields (workspace, feature, amount) decide whether a replay is the same request — but only the PRE-CHECK enforced it, on the row it could see. Two calls that both looked before either wrote took a different path entirely: one won the unique index, and the loser's `catch` established only that the collision was on `idempotencyKey` before reporting a successful replay, WITHOUT anything ever comparing the two requests. Two genuinely different requests sharing one key concurrently therefore produced one recording and two callers believing their own had been made — free quota, invisible in the counters, and refused by the sequential path on the way in. That is the worst shape a rule can have: it appeared to hold every time it was tested. `refund` had no collision path at all, so its retried duplicate surfaced a raw database violation for a movement that HAD been recorded. Both paths now read the WINNING event and compare it by the single `sameUsageRequest` rule the pre-check uses — a different request is `CONFLICT` however it arrived, the same one replays (consume) or no-ops (refund), and a collision with no readable winner re-throws the original error rather than inventing a verdict. The regression tests force the race with a held-open uncommitted transaction and wait on `pg_stat_activity` until the racer is genuinely blocked on the index, because committing on a bare `then()` let the loser take the sequential path about one run in three — a race test that is sometimes not a race is a test that will miss the defect it exists for. | Current execution Phase 3, pre-merge review | `packages/entitlements/src/usage.ts`, `tests/isolation/quota-enforcement.test.ts` |
| **D-250** | **AN ABUSE CEILING IS A SECOND DIMENSION, NOT A BIGGER LOCKOUT (F-19, closed).** Both realms locked an ACCOUNT after ten failed attempts and nothing counted the SOURCE, so an attacker spreading three attempts each across a thousand accounts met no brake at all, and one who wanted a named person locked out simply failed ten times on purpose. Signup and password reset had no ceiling of any kind: a probe on unmodified main issued TWENTY-FIVE working password-reset links for one address in a loop, from one unauthenticated caller. `AuthRateLimiter` counts both dimensions in one place — per source and per account — for sign-in, signup, password reset, verification resend and the second factor, and lives INSIDE `CustomerAuthService` and `SignupService` rather than in a router, because the product signs people in through a Next.js server action while the API has its own routes for the same operations and the two already disagreed about what they passed. One statement does the counting (`INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE … RETURNING`), so concurrent attempts contend on the unique key and no increment is lost — the R-01 shape, not repeated. It is POSTGRESQL rather than the Redis R-07 named: this counter is the record of an attack in progress, it must survive a restart, and it must be exactly right under concurrency rather than approximately right. **IT FAILS CLOSED**: a counter that cannot be written REFUSES, which costs nothing real because every one of these operations needs the same database one statement later — and the alternative is the failure docs/SECURITY.md §19.1 names, rate limiting silently switching itself off. The subject is stored as a HASH, so a dump of the table names neither who has an account nor where they connect from, and the ceilings are CONFIGURATION in the `onboarding` document, not constants. | Current execution Phase 4 | `packages/auth/src/rate-limit.ts`, `packages/config/src/domains.ts` |
| **D-251** | **A SECURITY EVENT WITH NO ORIGIN IS NOT AN INVESTIGABLE EVENT.** Two halves of one defect. The API built Fastify with no `trustProxy`, so `req.ip` was the transport peer — behind a load balancer, the balancer, identical for every request on earth — and wrote that into every audit row and every reset token. The DASHBOARD, which is the only surface a browser actually reaches, passed no address and no user agent at all: `signInAction`, `requestPasswordResetAction`, `verifyMfaAction`, `signUpAction` and `resendVerificationAction` each called a service that accepts both and supplied neither, so `audit_event.ip` and `audit_event.userAgent` were null on every authentication row the product has ever written. `requestContext()` in `@brandspace/shared` is the one reader for both apps, and it counts the forwarded chain FROM THE RIGHT by a declared hop count — `X-Forwarded-For` is a list the client can start, so trusting its leftmost entry lets a caller choose the identity a rate limiter counts. **THE DEFAULT IS TO TRUST NOTHING**: with no `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` the header is not read, because a developer running locally, or an operator who forgot, must not silently accept a client-chosen address. **CORRECTED IN REVIEW, AND THE CONTRACT WAS WRONG BEFORE THE INDEX WAS.** The first implementation read `chain.length - hops - 1`, and its tests encoded the same mistaken belief — that with one trusted proxy the chain reads `client, proxy`. A proxy appends the address of the PEER THAT CONNECTED TO IT, never its own, so with proxies P1…PN the header is `<client-supplied>, C, P1, …, P(N-1)`: exactly N appended entries, the FIRST of which is the client, at `chain.length - N`. With ONE proxy the client is the RIGHTMOST entry. The off-by-one therefore selected the last value the CLIENT supplied: a caller sending `X-Forwarded-For: 9.9.9.9` was identified as 9.9.9.9 and could pick a new one per request — a fresh rate-limit budget every time, which is the exact attack the hop count exists to stop. The same error made an ordinary one-entry chain look too short, so the DASHBOARD — where a server action has no socket address to fall back to — resolved no address at all and the per-source ceiling was skipped on the one path every browser takes. The tests are now built by a helper that PERFORMS the appends rather than asserting a hand-written string, because a hand-written chain can only confirm whatever its author already believed; and the spoofing property is asserted against the real limiter and a real database, not only against the helper. A skipped source dimension is now logged rather than silent. | Current execution Phase 4 | `packages/shared/src/request-context.ts`, `apps/dashboard/src/server/customer-context.ts`, `apps/api/src/routes/account.ts` |
| **D-252** | **THE OUTCOME OF A SECURITY EVENT IS THE TRUTH, NOT A CONSTANT.** `CustomerAuthService` wrote every audit event with `outcome: 'DENIED'` and a WARNING severity — including `customer.mfa.verified`, which records a SUCCESSFUL second factor. An operator filtering the trail for denials to find an attack found every successful MFA challenge mixed in, and an operator asking whether somebody got in was told no. Callers now say which it was; a failed counter write is `ERROR` rather than a denial of somebody, because nobody was denied by it. The same change gave the writer a user-agent parameter, which the column has waited for since Phase 1. | Current execution Phase 4 | `packages/auth/src/customer-session.ts` |
| **D-253** | **A SEND THAT FAILED IS NOT A SEND THAT WORKED — AND BOTH BRANCHES MUST SEND.** `requestPasswordResetAction` and `resendVerificationAction` caught every failure and redirected to their success page, so a provider outage told every customer their link was on its way and delivered none; both providers throw precisely so a caller can tell, and both callers discarded it. Surfacing the failure then introduced a NEW defect, which the regression test caught before it shipped: a registered address reached the provider and an unregistered one did not, so during an outage the two responses differed — an account-existence oracle created by the fix. The resolution is the pairing signup already uses: a registered address gets a reset link, an unregistered one gets a _there is no account here_ notice, both through the same provider on the same request, so the caller cannot tell which happened however the transport behaves. The per-account reset ceiling from D-250 is what keeps that from being a way to mail somebody repeatedly. **AND THE PAIRING ONLY WORKS IF BOTH HALVES CAN ACTUALLY BE DELIVERED**: the API's internal delivery route carried its own hand-written copy of the template set, so the new half was refused there and the fix was an oracle again wherever delivery is delegated rather than written to the outbox — which is every E2E run. `EMAIL_TEMPLATE_KEYS` is now the one runtime list, the union is derived from it and the route's schema is built from it (F-88). | Current execution Phase 4 | `apps/dashboard/src/app/[locale]/(auth)/actions.ts`, `packages/auth/src/email-templates.ts` |
| **D-254** | **CUSTOMER MFA WAS COMPLETE AND UNREACHABLE.** Enrolment, a live-code confirmation, hashed recovery codes consumed exactly once by a conditional update, and a disable that demands a working code have all existed and been tested since Phase 9 — and nothing in the product called any of it. The only callers were three API routes the dashboard never uses, so the sole way for a customer to enrol was for somebody to craft an HTTP request by hand with their session cookie; `remainingRecoveryCodes`, whose own comment calls it _the number the settings page shows_, had no caller at all. Phase 4 adds the screen, as an APPROVED DESIGN-SYSTEM EXTENSION under CLAUDE.md §4.2 — the approved demo has no security screen, and a missing reference must not block a functional gap. It is gated on NO permission, like `/permissions`: a second factor belongs to the person, and no workspace administrator has authority over another member's authenticator. `regenerateRecoveryCodes` is new and is the customer-side answer to F-13: codes are shown once and each works once, so somebody who spends them had no way back. | Current execution Phase 4 | `apps/dashboard/src/app/[locale]/settings/security/`, `packages/auth/src/signup.ts` |
| **D-255** | **A DOUBLE REFUSES ITSELF; A FACTORY REFUSING IT IS NOT ENOUGH.** CLAUDE.md §2.2 states that five development doubles refuse to be constructed or registered in production and names `assertNotProduction()` as how one joins them. Two had the call. The filesystem object store, the development payment provider and the two mock connector families relied on a FACTORY or the integrations registry refusing — correct for the one path that goes through it, and no protection at all against a `new` anywhere else. The local development key provider was the sharpest case: its own doc comment said `assertNotProduction()` refused it in production and the call was not there. Every one now carries its own, the factory refusals are unchanged and still first, and the comment now describes what the file actually does. Separately, `pnpm db:seed` — which creates a Platform Owner, enrols a second factor and prints it, and provisions two demonstration workspaces — had NO guard, while the throwaway E2E seeds each had one; it now calls the shared helper before it constructs a client. | Current execution Phase 4 | `packages/{storage,vault,billing,social-connectors,analytics}/src/…`, `packages/database/prisma/seed.ts` |

| **D-256** | **THE CLIENT-ORIGIN CONTRACT IS DECLARED, AND PRODUCTION WILL NOT START WITHOUT IT.** The live deployment was verified to carry NO origin configuration on either consuming service, which under the shipped reading meant `requestContext` established no address and `AuthRateLimiter.enforce` skipped any dimension with no subject — so every unauthenticated customer request in production ran with the per-ACCOUNT ceiling alone and NO per-source ceiling at all, silently, while looking entirely healthy. `CLIENT_ORIGIN_STRATEGY` now names the contract and `dashboard` and `api` refuse to boot in production without it; the other three services never establish a customer's source address and are deliberately not given it. **THE VALUE IS `railway-edge`, AND A HOP COUNT IS THE WRONG PRIMITIVE ON RAILWAY**: Railway's edge proxy strips a client-supplied `X-Forwarded-For` and writes the real connecting address FIRST, and the number of internal hops VARIES with the routing path because the CDN layer adds one and is not always present — so any fixed right-counted index is correct only some of the time, which for a security control is worse than being obviously wrong. `X-Real-IP` was considered and REJECTED: Railway sets it, but documents it as currently carrying the CDN edge address rather than the client's when the CDN is in the path, and a header that is right most of the time and silently wrong behind a CDN is the worst possible input to a rate limiter. Malformed configuration is REFUSED rather than coerced — the previous reader turned `''`, `-3`, `1.5`, `1abc` and `900` into a safe-looking number, and `0` means "read no header". The runtime invariant: a production request either has a trustworthy source identity the limiter counts, or is generically refused; it never proceeds with no source budget. **TIGHTENED IN REVIEW, ON THREE POINTS.** (1) `railway-edge` has NO SOCKET FALLBACK: the transport peer behind the edge is a Railway proxy — on the API it is `req.ip`, which is infrastructure and never a customer — so substituting it when the header is absent would collapse unrelated customers into ONE shared rate-limit subject, which is worse than no subject at all because it turns a missing signal into a wrong one. A missing or unusable chain answers `undefined` and production refuses. `xff-hops` KEEPS its socket fallback, and the asymmetry is the point: nothing strips the header there, so the peer is the last value the client could not have written. (2) THE HOP COUNT AND THE STRATEGY ARE VALIDATED AS A PAIR, in the schema rather than the production-only path, because `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` is not independently valid — it is required under `xff-hops` and must be ABSENT under the other two. `railway-edge` with `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS=` previously parsed cleanly and told an operator, wrongly, that a hop count was in force: a start-up pass with a request-time surprise. `requestContext` correspondingly does not parse the variable unless the effective strategy is `xff-hops`, so one the strategy ignores can never fail a request. (3) THE FILE'S COMMENTS NO LONGER CONTRADICT THEMSELVES: they had kept the universal 'the leftmost entry is client-controlled, count from the right' rule, which is the `xff-hops` contract and the exact opposite of `railway-edge`. Three strategies are now described separately. | Current execution Phase 4, owner review | `packages/shared/src/{env,request-context}.ts`, `packages/auth/src/rate-limit.ts`, `.railway/railway.ts` |

| **D-257** | **A PROVIDER CACHE KEYED ON TIME ALONE IS A CROSS-PROCESS LIE.** `apps/api` memoised the resolved email provider for sixty seconds with no reference to the configuration it was built from — and the process that ACTIVATES a provider is the Control Center while the process that SENDS is the API. An owner could connect Resend, watch the admin process invalidate its own configuration cache and report success, and have the API go on writing to the development outbox. **THE FAILURE IS SILENT**: `/v1/internal/email/deliver` returns 200 throughout, because a provider did accept the message, so the first evidence is a customer who never received a verification link. `resetEmailProviderCache()` existed for exactly this and HAD NO CALLERS anywhere — a cross-process cache cannot be invalidated by an in-process function call, so the design was unfixable in that shape.
